6.2.2
Discretization Error Analysis
The next lemma determines sufficiently sized ℓ∞balls to truncate ⃗X, ⃗W, ⃗Y, and ⃗Z to ensure a truncation
error of ϵtrunc. The goal is for there to be a O (poly(T, d, log(1/ϵ))) dependence on the point of truncation,
where the asymptotic quantities are T, d and 1/ϵ. The reason for this is that, due to the exponential nature
of the process, the number of bits used for discretization will end up depending on directly on the point of
truncation.
It turns out that we need some conditions on the parameters of the process to ensure that our upper bound
is even finite. This comes from that fact that we are integrating the exponential of a subexponential process.
The Heston model is known to experience so called “moment explosion” (mostly for moments greater than
one) issues for certain parameter regimes, payoff functions (especially super-linear payoffs), and simulation
times [AP07].
Lemma 6.1 (Heston Truncation Error). Consider a multi-dimensional Heston model in Definition 6.1 evolving to
a time T. Suppose the model parameters (for each asset) satisfy the following conditions:

The following theorem using Lemma 6.1 to determine how the number of (qu)bits needs to scale to obtain
an ϵdisc discretization error for numerical integration applied to 6.15.
Theorem 6.5 (Heston Discretization Error). Under the assumptions of Lemmas 6.1 along with ηk ≥5, it suffices

O

T4d2 log3/2(Td/ϵtrunc) log(Td/ϵdisc)

(qu)bits for an ϵdisc discretization error.
The proof (in Appendix D.4) follows from applying the left-endpoint rule (Lemma F.3) to Equation (6.14)
over the region defined by Lemma 6.1. In this setting, we are unable to get a bound on the number of bits
per primitive distribution that scales only logarithmically in Td. Additionally, we need to leave a small
region of ⃗x (Lemma D.2) near zero to avoid singularities in the derivatives of (6.14). The challenge in doing
this comes from the lack of a closed-form expression for the pdf of ⃗X. Instead, we utilize a hitting-time
bound that appears in the proof of the Feller condition. The result, unfortunately, leads to an exponential
blow-up in the asymptotics of the derivatives in terms of Td.
An immediate corollary of Theorems 6.4 and 6.5 are that the we can obtain an end-to-end quadratic quan-
tum speedup for pricing derivatives over the version of the Heston model in Definition 6.1.
Theorem 6.6 (Quantum Speedup for Pricing Over Heston Model). Suppose ( f, bS) is a Financial Derivative
Model (Definition 2.1), where is f be a piecewise linear, B-Lipschitz payoff function, and bS is d-dimensional Heston
Model (Definition 6.1) asset path-processes bS initialized at (⃗S(0), ⃗V(0)) evolved over T time steps. Then, there is
a quantum algorithm using eO (poly(d, T, B)/ϵ) one- and two-qubit gates, O(poly(T)) qubits, and outputs an
ϵ-additive estimate of E[ f (bS)|bS(0) = s0, bV(0) = v0] with constant probability.
Note that f can also be a function of bV, which reduces to the CIR case, Section 6.1.
6.2.3
Discussion on Model Parameter Constraints
In this section we investigate the implications of the constraints we put on the parameters on the finiteness
of the Heston moments. A previous result from the literature shows the following conditions on these
parameters for E[S(t)ω] to be finite, where ω ∈R+. To apply the below result to a path process, like
Equation (6.4), consider setting r = t∆for t ∈[T]:
Proposition 6.1 (Finite-time Moment Explosion Conditions [AP07, Proposition 3.1]). Consider a stochastic
process S(r) following the Heston SDEs given by Definition 2.3. Consider ω > 1, then the ω-th moment of S(r), i.e.
E[Sω(r)], is finite for all r ∈[0, T ) and infinite for r ≥T , where

7
Speedups with Quantum MLMC for Correlated Processes
In this section, we tackle applying QMCI to models that lack fast-forwardability. In this case, the SDE evo-
lution can only be approximated to error ϵ with a number of steps growing like O (poly(1/ϵ)). We refer
the reader to Section 3.1.4 for a review of approximate SDE simulation (via Itˆo-Taylor schemes) and clas-
sical/quantum multi-level Monte Carlo (MLMC). Multi-level schemes provide the state-of-the-art asymp-
totic complexity in this regime, and can achieve a total sample complexity that is comparable to vanilla
(Q)MCI. We will demonstrate that quantum MLMC can achieve an end-to-end quantum speedup over clas-
sical vanilla and multi-level Monte Carlo, when the SDE needs to be simulated using a Itˆo-Taylor scheme
and in the presence of correlations. The types of correlations that we consider are “bipartite” and can be
reduced to the task of (n, 2)-Milstein sampling (see Section 7.1 for definition). In Section 7.2 we examine the
inner workings of MLMC and, unlike previous studies, specifically elucidate the role played by the errors
discussed in Section 4.2.
We will demonstrate the speedup by showing that a subroutine (Section 7.3), which we coin the quantum
Milstein sampler, is a sufficient proxy for the Milstein scheme. When combined with the results of An et
al. [An+21], i.e. Theorem 3.3, this will imply that we have an end-to-end quadratic speedup (Theorem 7.2)
with quantum MLMC for pricing derivatives with Lipschitz payoffs over models with bipartite correla-
tions.
Unfortunately, it is still unclear if quantum MCI can achieve a quadratic speedup for models with arbitrary
correlations. This is because the existing methods for multi-dimensional L´evy area sampling are insuffi-
cient. Specifically, these methods scale roughly as MCI, i.e γ in Theorem 3.2 would become too large if we
need to embed an approximate L´evy area sampler inside the Milstein scheme. Additionally, the existing
methods are known to be optimal when one is given access to only the Brownian increments [Dic07; FH23].
47


## Page 48


To emphasize the challenges with multi-dimensional L´evy area sampling, the existing literature has started
to move to use more practical methods like deep generative models [Jel+23]. Hence, new algorithmic tech-
niques would be require to sample L´evy areas more efficiently and obtain theoretical guarantees.
7.1
Multi-dimensional L´evy area Sampling
One consequence of the results in [An+21] is that quantum MLMC can achieve a quadratic speedup for
globally Lipschitz payoffs, provided that a sufficiently high-order numerical scheme is employed. Unlike
classical MLMC, the quantum approach requires higher-order schemes, which become significantly harder
to implement in the presence of correlations. Specifically, it appears that a strong-order one scheme is
generally required for quantum MLMC. While this condition is currently only sufficient, as mentioned
in Section 1.2 (Contribution 4), it also seems to be necessary, as a consequence of the quadratic variance
reduction provided by quantum. Our contribution demonstrates that quantum MLMC retains an end-to-
end speedup even when some correlations are present.
It is known that the Euler-Maruyama scheme (Equation (3.8)) attains the optimal order of strong conver-
gence ( 1
2) given access to only the increments of the multi-dimensional Wiener process ⃗Wt [CC05; Dic07].
To achieve a faster order of convergence, one must look to higher-order schemes [Pla99, Theorem 10.6.3],
which, in general, require sampling iterated stochastic integrals (in this case Itˆo integrals):
I⃗j(h) =
Z h
0 · · ·
Z s3
0
Z s2
0
dWj1(s1)dWj2(s2) · · · dWjm(sm),
where Wjk are the “spatial” components of the multi-dimensional Brownian motion ⃗W. To perform time-
discretization schemes for SDEs, we desire being able to sample from the joint distribution of the increments
and iterated stochastic integrals:
(∆W1, . . . ∆Wm, . . . I(j1,j2), . . . , I(jm−1,jm), . . . , I⃗j),
which involves 2m random variables. Like in the deterministic case, the (strong) Itˆo-Taylor time-discretization
schemes form a hierarchy of increasingly accurate, yet more computationally expensive, approximations [Pla99,
Theorem 5.5.1]. Luckily, for classical and quantum MLMC and at least globally-Lipschitz payoffs, we only
need to consider at most double integrals. However, unfortunately, these can still be quite challenging to
sample from.
The Milstein scheme [Pla99, Theorem 10.3.5] with strong order of convergence of 1 only requires sampling
Brownian increments and double stochastic integrals:
Ijk(h) =

Hence, we only require estimating m + (m
2) random variables. It is well-known that the Milstein scheme
can avoid computing L´evy areas if either (1) there are no correlations between processes or (2) the processes
satisfy the commutativity condition (Section 3.1.4). This commutativity condition appears to be hard to
satisfy for general multi-dimensional models used in finance, such as the multi-asset Heston.
Giles [GS14] proposed an antithetic sampling approach that can still avoid L´evy areas. It appears unclear
how to use this approach quantumly, so we do not discuss it any further. It is also possible to bypass
this requirement on computing L´evy areas if a different metric is used. For example, it is known that a
strong-order one scheme in Wasserstein-2 metric exists without sampling iterated integrals [Dav14]. Un-
fortunately, in our case, we will require something stronger, i.e. bounds on L2 error.
48


## Page 49


We will refer to the task of (n, m)-L´evy sampling of an n-dimensional Brownian Motion as sampling from
the joint distribution of the random variables associated with some set S ⊆[n] × [n]:
A(n,m) := {∆W1, . . . ∆Wn} ∪{A(j,k) : (j, k) ∈S ⊂[n] × [n], j < k, |S| = m},
and where dWj · dWk = 0, ∀j, k ≤n. We will pay special attention to the (n, 2) case, which is sometimes
referred to as two-dimensional L´evy area sampling.
It is known that the KL expansion [Pla99, Section 5.8] is optimal for approximate (n, m)-L´evy area sampling
given only access to Brownian increments (∆Wj), with error scaling as O(N−1/2) [Dic07; FH23], in terms
of the number of Gaussian samples. The use of such approximations would cause the cost of simulation,
γ, in Theorem 3.2, to be too large. This would then imply an even faster converging scheme would be
required to retain the speedup. Furthermore, it seems that a convergence rate of O(N−1/2) in L2 for L´evy
area approximation is not sufficient to retain the convergence rate of the Milstein scheme (Corollary 10.6.5
[Pla99]).
For the moment, lets ignore the last comment and consider the cost of Milstein simulation using the KL
scheme, with the goal of retaining the O(h−1) strong-convergence rate. The sample cost of simulation with
step size h goes as O(h−5), γ = 5, which is significantly worse than the cost of Euler-Maruyama with
O(h−1), γ = 1. The proof that Milstein is sufficient for QMCI to retain its quadratic speedup needs γ to
remain ≤1. This implies it is unclear from the results of [An+21] whether quantum can retain even an
end-to-end speedup when L´evy areas need to be computed.
It turns out that the pdf for (n, 2)-L´evy area sampling is known in closed form [L´ev51]. This enabled Gaines
and Lyons [GL94] to develop an efficient numerical procedure for sampling from
(∆W1, ∆W2, A(1,2)),
which is (2, 2)-L´evy area sampling. The authors did not perform an asymptotic analysis of the procedure.
However, Corollary 5.3 in Section 5.2.2 shows how to quantumly sample form (2, 2)-L´evy areas. It should
be apparent that the (n, 2) case is just the product of the densities of the (n
2), (2, 2) cases.
If we are considering an n-dimensional model where any given process is correlated with at most one other
process, then, as shown below, sampling from the n-dimensional Milstein scheme only requires (n, 2) L´evy
area sampling. We refer to this task as (n, 2)-Milstein Sampling and say the process has bipartite correla-
tions.
It is important to mention that for MLMC, we actually require something weaker than ensuring that the
approximate Milstein scheme retains its O(h) strong convergence rate. Hence, ensuring that we have an L2
approximation to the L´evy areas may not be necessary (as assumed in Corollary 10.6.5 [Pla99]). This fact
will be apparent from the derivations of the results in Section 7.2 and is what enables us to get a speedup
with the above approach for the (n, 2) case. Still, as presented earlier, the KL approach is too expensive to
retain the speedup.
In the next section, we analyze the role of the various sources of error from Section 4.2 within the MLMC
framework, which has not been done previously. Then, in Section 7.3 , using the quantum L´evy area sam-
pler introduced in Section 5, we present an efficient (O (poly log(1/ϵ))) scheme for (n, 2)-Milstein qsam-
pling. Additionally, we analyze the impact of the distribution error from the quantum Milstein sampler.
Together, these results show that the error produced by the Milstein scheme with the approximate L´evy area
sampler does not destroy the quadratic speedup provided by quantum MLMC (obtained with the ideal Mil-
stein). When combined with quantum MLMC (Theorem 3.3), our result shows that quantum computation
does provide an end-to-end speedup for derivative pricing in the setting of approximate SDE simulation
with some correlations.

7.2
Error Analysis for Quantum MLMC
While it was not addressed in [An+21], we need to account for the various sources of error discussed in
Section 4.2 within the MLMC framework. This necessary for showing that our quantum Milstein sampler
is compatible with quantum MLMC. To do this, we need to recall some of the components of MLMC,
following Giles [Gil08], and extend the analysis to include other sources of error.
Like usual, suppose our payoff f depends on T, d-dimensional monitoring points separated by a time
increment of ∆, forming a path process bX. However, to ensure the approximation error is sufficiently low,
we will need to simulate X(t) at points that are in-between monitoring points. Suppose we apply an SDE
discretization scheme to simulate the continuous-time process X(t) with step-size hℓ= 2−ℓ∆. This will
result in approximating a 2ℓT-length, d-dimensional path process, which we denote by bY. The payoff f
will only depend on the points bY(2ℓr), r ∈[T]. We define eYℓto be the approximation of bY output by the
discretization scheme with “level” ℓ, which takes hℓ= 2−ℓ∆.
The MLMC framework considers an estimator (obtained via Θ(L) (Q)MCI runs) for the quantity

process.

Quantum PDE Solvers for Distribution Loading
As mentioned in Section 1.1, one of the main limitations of the existing quantum derivative pricing frame-
work is that the quantum sampling or loading of the discrete sum utilizes a number of qubits that scale at
53


## Page 54


least linearly with T, where T is the number of monitoring points for a path-dependent derivative (e.g. ap-
proach mentioned in Section 3.1.3). The quantity T is typically not considered to be an asymptotic quantity,
but it can still be large. Hence, savings in T can have substantial practical benefits, as qubits are a precious
resource.
One class of state preparation techniques that can utilize fewer than poly(T) qubits are quantum PDE
solvers. Specifically, if a T-dimension state can be expressed as the solution to a PDE then, in some cases,
quantum computers can prepare the state using only poly(log T) qubits. For example, it is well-known that
the marginals of an Itˆo SDE follow the Fokker-Planck PDE (reviewed in Section 8.1). Hence the distribution
can be loaded onto a quantum state using a quantum PDE solver, which we outline how to do in Section
8.2.
Unfortunately, in Section 8.3, we show that the quantum PDE distribution-loading procedure is not compati-
ble with quantum algorithms for derivative pricing, for a variety of reasons. We will discuss significant obstacles
to this approach. Thus, it currently seems open as to whether one can obtain sublinear space quantum sam-
pling from classical SDEs in a way that is compatible with (Monte Carlo based) quantum derivative pricing
algorithms.
Beyond PDE solvers, there have been a few other techniques proposed for quantumly accelerating the sim-
ulation of classical stochastic processes in either space or time. One such case was shown by Apers and
Sarlette [AS19], where they showed discrete-time quantum walks can be used to approximately simulate
symmetric Markov chains in sublinear time. One issue with this approach is that it incurs a subnormaliza-
tion penalty as we are directly amplitude encoding probability vectors, not their squares (this issue also oc-
curs with quantum PDE solvers, Section 8.3.2). The second is that the chain needs to be symmetric, which is
unlikely for financial processes. The second is by Prakash et al. [Pra+24] who utilized the Karhunen-Lo`eve
expansion to accelerate the simulation of GBM in space and time. However, using this subroutine within
QMCI results in additional factors of 1
ϵ. Thus, all of these approaches appear to have challenges of their
own.
Lastly, our proposed usage of quantum PDE solvers as a subroutine for pricing is distinct from other usages
in the existing literature, and is motivated by our focus on Monte Carlo based pricing. An alternative pric-
ing approach to QMCI is to use quantum PDE solvers to solve a PDE (e.g. the Black-Scholes or Backward
Kolmogorov PDE) for the price itself, like in Ref. [MK21]. One of the major issues with such approaches is
the need to extract the price (encoded as an amplitude) from the quantum state. This is well known already
to have at least an inverse-polynomial dependence on the desired precision, and hence comparable to clas-
sical and quantum MCI. The allure of such methods is to overcome the exponential dimension dependence
present in classical PDE solvers. However, as we have shown (e.g., Section 6), Monte Carlo methods for
pricing common derivatives already have a polynomial dependence on dimension. Hence, the current ben-
efits of this approach remain unclear. Additionally some of the challenges found in this paper apply to this
setting.

One can then prepare a quantum state encoding the solution to this system using quantum linear sys-
tems algorithms [HHL09; Cos+21]. These utilize O(κ log(1/ϵ)) queries to an oracle for the initial state
and matrix, where κ is the condition number of the stencil. The number of additional gates and qubits is
O(poly(log(T), log(n), d)). The whole thing has O(poly(log(T), log(n), d) · κ log(1/ϵ)) gate complexity if
we can prepare the initial state and access the stencil efficiently.
8.3
Challenges in Monte Carlo Pricing with Quantum PDE solvers
Here we provide three significant barriers to utilizing quantum PDE solvers as distribution loading sub-
routines for quantum MCI.
8.3.1
Insufficiency of History States for Path-Dependent Derivative Pricing
The output of the quantum PDE solver applied to the FP equation is a superposition encoding the marginals
of the Markov chain at each time step, which we call a history state.
Definition 8.1 (History State). Let {X(t) | 0 ≤t ≤T} be a stochastic process over a space X such that the
probability density of X(t) at any time t is given by pt : X →R≥0. Under some suitable choice of discretization, let
the quantum state with amplitudes proportional to pα/2

corresponding history state is defined as

The reason for only encoding marginals comes from the definition of the Fokker-Planck equation, and
the need to encode the history comes from the need to unitarily-embed the FP dynamics. We present the
following result regarding the inability to price path-dependent derivatives given access to only history
states.
Lemma 8.1. There exist stochastic processes X1(t), X2(t) over the same ”discrete” domain X and a starting point
x0, such that the ”history” states (8.1) corresponding to running X1(t) and X2(t) for time T, starting from x0 are
identical, and a barrier payoff function F such that the price computed by integrating F over the paths {X1(t)|0 ≤
t ≤T} and {X2(t)|0 ≤t ≤T} differs by greater than c, where c is a universal constant.
Proof. We choose any discrete set X without loss of generality and x0 some element of X . Define an arbi-

Define the two stochastic processes as follows
• At t = 0, X1 makes a uniformly random step on to the whole domain. At every subsequent step, X1
deterministically does not make any move from any point of the domain (the transition matrix is the
identity).
• X2 makes a uniformly random step on to the whole domain at every time step.
57


## Page 58


It is easy to see that at t = 0 both stochastic processes have the marginal distribution δ(x −x0) and at every
subsequent time step the marginal distributions for both distributions are uniform over X0. However, the
integral of the payoff function over the distribution of the paths differs by more than a constant if the
stochastic processes are run for time Θ(log |X |). To see this, we notice that for X1, half the paths jump to
points in X ′ at t = 1 and after time t = 1 every path is stationary. Thus the corresponding price is 1/2.
On the other hand, every path in X2 jumps to an independent new point in X at each time step. After
Θ(| log |X |) steps, it is overwhelmingly likely (say with probability > 0.99) that any individual path enters
X ′ at some point. The expected price equals the probability that the payoff for any given path is 1, and thus
the payoff corresponding to X2 is at least 0.99.
The above lemma shows that in general, history states may be completely insufficient for the computation
of integrals over paths even when given any finite number of states as a resource, or allowed any finite
number of calls to a state preparation oracle for the quantum state. However, the distributions in Lemma 8.1
are quite different from typical stochastic processes encountered in finance. In particular, the distribution
is over a discrete support, and the parameters describing the distribution are rapidly changing with time.
We next show that these properties are in fact unnecessary and there are continuous stochastic processes
that exhibit similar properties for a properly chosen barrier option. These examples are based on the same
essential idea as above, namely that the marginal distributions of a stochastic process do not fully capture
its transition dynamics. Note that these generic lower bound arguments cannot be made for an arbitrary
distribution: as we have seen, payoffs with dependence only on the marginals such as path-independent
payoffs can in fact be evaluated using only history states.
Lemma 8.2. There exist stochastic processes ⃗X1, ⃗X2 on the domain Rd described by stochastic differential equa-
tions d⃗Xj = ⃗µ(⃗Xj, t)dt + σ(⃗Xj, t)d⃗Wt for j = 1, 2, such that the drift and volatility coefficients are all Lipschitz
continuous, and the corresponding ”history” states (8.1) have a trace distance of ≤ϵ = 1/2Ω(d). Additionally,
there is a time-dependent barrier payoff function F such that the price computed by integrating F over the paths
{⃗X1(t)|0 ≤t ≤T} and {⃗X2(t)|0 ≤t ≤T} differs by greater than c, where c is a universal constant.
Proof. Let α, b, σ be real parameters such that 2αb ≥σ2. Define the stochastic processes X1, X2 as follows:
dX1(t) = α(b −X(t)) dt + σ

the path. The empirical covariances of X1, X2 are concentrated around values whose difference is bounded
below as T →∞. Setting K to be between these two values, we can ensure that the expected payoff value
differs by more than a constant.
8.3.2

[AS19]. More specifically, the quantum PDE solver for the Fokker-Planck prepares

does not need to prepare the | f ⟩state and (2) has a dependence on f and pT that is more like pT · f, which
can be substantially smaller than QIPE’s.
Alternatively, one could transform the FP PDE into a nonlinear one in order to produce a qsample, i.e. |p1⟩.
However, quantum nonlinear PDE solvers have stringent conditions under which they work efficiently
[Liu+21].
8.3.3
Insufficient Runtime with Quantum PDE Solvers
The matrices that arise as discretizations of PDEs are typically sparse and have efficiently row-computable
entries, which makes them amenable to quantum linear-algebra algorithms. Unfortunately, quantum algo-
rithms, like classical iterative methods, operate on the spectrum of the system matrix and thus have a poly-
nomial dependence on the condition number. It is not clear how to sufficiently bound this condition number
for the task of quantum Monte Carlo integration. This is because any additional inverse-polynomial depen-
dence in the error can destroy the quantum speedup. Preconditioning may be one way to elleviate this issue
[RKT25]. In addition, quantum PDE solvers can suffer a substantial slowdown when the PDE infinitesimal
generator is not a normal operator [An+22b].
An alternative to FDM that is applicable to certain classes of PDEs are the pseudo-spectral methods [CLO21].
A pseudospectral method approximates the solution to the PDEs using polynomial approximations, which
results in a system of ordinary differential equations. The Chebyshev spectral method is particularly ef-
ficient for quantum due to quantum Fourier transform. In the case of FP, once the time discretiation has
been applied, under certain conditions the result is a system of Elliptic PDEs. Unfortunately, there are
are additional spectral constraints [CLO21], such as diagonal dominance, that are required for the quan-
tum algorithm to be efficient. This condition appears to be too strong to assume in general for financial
applications.

Next let us determine how many qubits are required for the output register, i.e. to hold the valeue f ◦gt.
Note that in (C.1), each time zt is squared, unless it is the final time point, it gets square-rooted in the
next step. Additionally, each xt−1 is square-rooted before squaring. Hence, ignoring the Newton error, the
required number of bits to hold the output of the recursion does not grow with time. So without loss of
generality, we can suppose gt requires O(log2(N)) bits to represent. The number of lower order bits is
O(log2(N/b)). Note that f can expand the output by at most a factor of O(BT). Hence O(log(BTN)) bits
also suffices for the output register.
The O(T) other additions and multiplications/scalings cost together O(T log(N)2) gates. Along with the
oracle for the payoff, we can perform the operation:

computes the t-th point in bS from the vector of path increments.

exponential in d or T.
